Yeah… I think I am. I mean, not always in the obvious way, but I’ll take the long way around the building so I don’t have to pass certain areas. And I avoid that floor whenever I can.

Yeah… yes. I mean, I don’t sit there trying to think about it, but it just kind of barges in.

Yeah… I guess there is. I try not to think about that whole stretch of it—the contractor, the cutover, all of that. Even just people bringing it up makes me want to get out of the conversation.

I don’t think so… not like I’m watching myself from the outside. I mean, sometimes afterward I’ll think, “Why did I do that?” like why did I walk all the way around instead of just going where I needed to go, but that’s more me realizing it later.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. “Numb” maybe isn’t the word I’d use… or maybe I’m overthinking the word, which I do a lot. My job is basically getting paid to argue with vague terminology, so if someone says “disconnected,” my brain wants a requirements document.

I don’t think I feel cut off from the world, exactly. I’m aware of where I am, who’s around, what I’m supposed to be doing. Sometimes I might get quiet or kind of… blank for a minute, but I don’t know if that’s numbness or just me trying not to react in front of people.

So I guess I haven’t really noticed feeling disconnected from myself, not clearly. It’s more like I’m trying to keep things contained.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I’ve started doing things that honestly scare me. Like, I’ll just walk out in the middle of a deployment at work, which is insane because I’m usually the person who stays until everything is stable. Or I’ll get in my car late at night and just drive around with no plan.\n\nI know that probably sounds kind of random, but I’ve always been pretty controlled, you know? I work in systems, so planning is kind of my whole thing. This is just… not how I usually handle stress, and it’s starting to make me nervous.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious before or while doing these impulsive actions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but it’s hard to separate. It’s not like I calmly decide, “I’m going to do something reckless now.” It’s more like I get this pressure building, and then suddenly I’m walking out during a deployment or I’m in the car late at night just driving with no plan.\n\nAnd that’s what scares me, because I’m not usually like that. I’m usually the person with the checklist and the backup plan and, you know, three tabs open to monitor everything. So while it’s happening I feel kind of wound up and not fully… I don’t know, not fully in control of it.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding anything or any situation when you do these impulsive actions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I am. I mean, not always in the obvious way, but I’ll take the long way around the building so I don’t have to pass certain areas. And I avoid that floor whenever I can.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous saying it out loud, because it’s just a floor, right? I used to go up and down there constantly for work. But now I’ll make excuses or suddenly remember I need coffee, or I’ll use the stairs on the other side. And if people start talking about the contractor or the cutover, I kind of… shut it down. I change the subject or leave the conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, I don’t sit there trying to think about it, but it just kind of barges in.\n\nEspecially if I’m near that conference area, or if someone on a call suddenly raises their voice—like not even at me, just that sharp tone people get when production is down—I’ll suddenly get these images and sounds from that night. It’s like they’re right there for a second, and I lose the thread of what I was saying.\n\nWhich is… embarrassing, honestly, because I’ll be mid-sentence about a ticket or some server issue, and then I’m just blank. People probably think I’m distracted, but it’s not that. It’s more like my brain yanks me somewhere else before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving that night, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes it really does feel like that, or close to it. It’s not like I’m choosing to remember it.\n\nI’ll be near that conference area, or someone on a call raises their voice—people do that all the time in tech when something breaks, you know, like suddenly everyone’s talking over each other—and then out of nowhere I get hit with the images and the sounds from that night. I try not to think about that whole stretch of it—the contractor, the cutover, all of that. Even just people bringing it up makes me want to get out of the conversation.\n\nI’ll do stupid little things, like take the long way around the building so I don’t have to pass by that area, or avoid that floor if I can. I know that sounds like I’m making a big production out of it, but I don’t even always notice I’m doing it until afterward. Sorry, that’s probably not answering it clearly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t sit there and say, “Okay, now I’m avoiding a memory,” but that’s basically what happens.

If something makes me think about that night, I try to get away from it. I’ll take the long way around the building just so I don’t have to go near that area, which is ridiculous because it adds time and then I’m annoyed at myself for being late to something I could’ve reached in two minutes. It’s like when the parking garage elevators are down and everyone suddenly becomes an urban planner, except I’m doing it on purpose.

And I avoid that floor whenever I can. If someone starts talking about the contractor, or the cutover, I’ll shut the conversation down fast. Change the subject, say I have another call, pretend I need to check something. I just don’t want it getting started in my head.

I try not to think about that whole stretch of it—the contractor, the cutover, all of that. Even just people bringing it up makes me want to get out of the conversation.\n\nI’ll do stupid little things, like take the long way around the building so I don’t have to pass by that area, or avoid that floor if I can. I know that sounds like I’m making a big production out of it, but I don’t even always notice I’m doing it until afterward. Like, suddenly I’ve gone all the way around just to avoid being near it.\n\nAnd if someone starts talking about that night, or the project, I kind of shut it down. Change the subject, remember I have an email to send, anything. I just don’t want it in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. I mean, I’ve definitely felt really uncomfortable suddenly, but “peaked within minutes” is… I don’t know, I don’t really time it.\n\nSometimes when something happens, I get thrown off pretty fast, but then I start thinking about whether I looked weird or if I need to get back to what I was doing, and then I’m worrying about the meeting itself. Sorry, that’s probably not answering it clearly. I guess I haven’t noticed a specific sudden episode like that, not in the way you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsive actions and avoidance behaviors are directly related to something specific that happened, or are they more about general feelings of anxiety or stress?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more tied to that specific thing, yeah. I mean, I get stressed like anyone does—work is always some disaster with systems and deadlines, and people act like everything is on fire because a server hiccuped—but this feels different.\n\nIt’s like when it gets near anything about the contractor or the cutover, I just… I don’t want to be anywhere near it. I’ll take the long way around the building without even fully deciding to, or avoid that floor if I can. And if someone starts talking about it, I shut it down pretty fast. Change the subject, find a reason to leave, whatever.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess it’s not just general anxiety. It’s connected to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself do things, when you're having these impulsive episodes or avoiding certain situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I’m watching myself from the outside. I mean, sometimes afterward I’ll think, “Why did I do that?” like why did I walk all the way around instead of just going where I needed to go, but that’s more me realizing it later.\n\nI’m usually still… in myself, I guess. Just trying to get through the moment or get away from the conversation. Though, honestly, when you say “outside my body,” I start thinking of those weird corporate training videos where they tell you to “observe your behavior objectively,” which always sounds nice until you’re actually in the middle of something and you’re just trying not to look strange.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the event from that night, like you're back in it again?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Which is funny—well, not funny, but you know, people on calls raise their voices all the time, especially when something breaks and everyone suddenly becomes an expert in infrastructure. But then I’ll hear it a certain way, and I get these images and sounds from that night, really strong, like they’re right there.\n\nAnd I’ll lose track of what I was saying. Like mid-sentence, just gone for a second. Then I’m trying to cover it, because nobody wants the systems analyst freezing up in the middle of a status update. But yes, I guess that is kind of like being back in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel numb or disconnected from yourself or the world around you when you're experiencing these memories or impulses?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Numb” maybe isn’t the word I’d use… or maybe I’m overthinking the word, which I do a lot. My job is basically getting paid to argue with vague terminology, so if someone says “disconnected,” my brain wants a requirements document.\n\nI don’t think I feel cut off from the world, exactly. I’m aware of where I am, who’s around, what I’m supposed to be doing. Sometimes I might get quiet or kind of… blank for a minute, but I don’t know if that’s numbness or just me trying not to react in front of people.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed feeling disconnected from myself, not clearly. Yeah… it has. I don’t love admitting that, because I’ve always been pretty reliable at work. Like, annoyingly reliable. The person people send the messy handoff to because I’ll make a spreadsheet out of the chaos.

Um… since the cutover. The late-night one.

I keep wanting to say it started “around then,” because that sounds less dramatic, but honestly it’s been pretty much since that incident. A little over a year now—about 13 months, I think. And I avoid that floor whenever I can.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous saying it out loud, because it’s just a floor, right? I used to go up and down there constantly for work. But now I’ll make excuses or suddenly remember I need coffee, or I’ll use the stairs on the other side. And if people start talking about the contractor or the cutover, I kind of… shut it down. I change the subject or leave the conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, I don’t sit there trying to think about it, but it just kind of barges in.\n\nEspecially if I’m near that conference area, or if someone on a call suddenly raises their voice—like not even at me, just that sharp tone people get when production is down—I’ll suddenly get these images and sounds from that night. It’s like they’re right there for a second, and I lose the thread of what I was saying.\n\nWhich is… embarrassing, honestly, because I’ll be mid-sentence about a ticket or some server issue, and then I’m just blank. People probably think I’m distracted, but it’s not that. It’s more like my brain yanks me somewhere else before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving that night, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes it really does feel like that, or close to it. It’s not like I’m choosing to remember it.\n\nI’ll be near that conference area, or someone on a call raises their voice—people do that all the time in tech when something breaks, you know, like suddenly everyone’s talking over each other—and then out of nowhere I get hit with the images and the sounds from that night. And for a second it’s so strong that I’m not really in the meeting anymore.\n\nI’ll just lose what I was saying. Like I’ll be explaining something normal, some ticket or system issue, and then it’s just… gone. I have to kind of pretend I was checking my notes or something. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about that night that you're trying to avoid thinking about or remembering?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there is. I try not to think about that whole stretch of it—the contractor, the cutover, all of that. Even just people bringing it up makes me want to get out of the conversation.\n\nI’ll do stupid little things, like take the long way around the building so I don’t have to pass by that area, or avoid that floor if I can. I know that sounds like I’m making a big production out of it, but I don’t even always notice I’m doing it until afterward. Like, suddenly I’ve gone all the way around just to avoid being near it.\n\nAnd if someone starts talking about that night, or the project, I kind of shut it down. Change the subject, remember I have an email to send, anything. I just don’t want it in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. I mean, I’ve definitely felt really uncomfortable suddenly, but “peaked within minutes” is… I don’t know, I don’t really time it.\n\nSometimes when something happens, I get thrown off pretty fast, but then I start thinking about whether I looked weird or if I need to get back to what I was doing, and then I’m worrying about the meeting itself. Sorry, that’s probably not answering it clearly. I guess I haven’t noticed a specific sudden episode like that, not in the way you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsive actions and avoidance behaviors are directly related to something specific that happened, or are they more about general feelings of anxiety or stress?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more tied to that specific thing, yeah. I mean, I get stressed like anyone does—work is always some disaster with systems and deadlines, and people act like everything is on fire because a server hiccuped—but this feels different.\n\nIt’s like when it gets near anything about the contractor or the cutover, I just… I don’t want to be anywhere near it. I’ll take the long way around the building without even fully deciding to, or avoid that floor if I can. And if someone starts talking about it, I shut it down pretty fast. Change the subject, find a reason to leave, whatever.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess it’s not just general anxiety. It’s connected to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself do things, when you're having these impulsive episodes or avoiding certain situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I’m watching myself from the outside. I mean, sometimes afterward I’ll think, “Why did I do that?” like why did I walk all the way around instead of just going where I needed to go, but that’s more me realizing it later.\n\nI’m usually still… in myself, I guess. Just trying to get through the moment or get away from the conversation. Though, honestly, when you say “outside my body,” I start thinking of those weird corporate training videos where they tell you to “observe your behavior objectively,” which always sounds nice until you’re actually in the middle of something and you’re just trying not to look strange.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the event from that night, like you're back in it again?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like I completely forget where I am, exactly, but out of nowhere I’ll get hit with it.\n\nIt happens more in that conference area, or if someone raises their voice on a call. Which is funny—well, not funny, but you know, people on calls raise their voices all the time, especially when something breaks and everyone suddenly becomes an expert in infrastructure. But then I’ll hear it a certain way, and I get these images and sounds from that night, really strong, like they’re right there.\n\nAnd I’ll lose track of what I was saying. Like mid-sentence, just gone for a second. Then I’m trying to cover it, because nobody wants the systems analyst freezing up in the middle of a status update. But yes, I guess that is kind of like being back in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel numb or disconnected from yourself or the world around you when you're experiencing these memories or impulses?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Numb” maybe isn’t the word I’d use… or maybe I’m overthinking the word, which I do a lot. My job is basically getting paid to argue with vague terminology, so if someone says “disconnected,” my brain wants a requirements document.\n\nI don’t think I feel cut off from the world, exactly. I’m aware of where I am, who’s around, what I’m supposed to be doing. Sometimes I might get quiet or kind of… blank for a minute, but I don’t know if that’s numbness or just me trying not to react in front of people.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed feeling disconnected from myself, not clearly. It’s more like I’m trying to keep things contained.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to push away or avoid thoughts and memories related to that night when they come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t sit there and say, “Okay, now I’m avoiding a memory,” but that’s basically what happens.\n\nIf something makes me think about that night, I try to get away from it. I’ll take the long way around the building just so I don’t have to go near that area, which is ridiculous because it adds time and then I’m annoyed at myself for being late to something I could’ve reached in two minutes. It’s like when the parking garage elevators are down and everyone suddenly becomes an urban planner, except I’m doing it on purpose.\n\nAnd I avoid that floor whenever I can. If someone starts talking about the contractor, or the cutover, I’ll shut the conversation down fast. Change the subject, say I have another call, pretend I need to check something. I just don’t want it getting started in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms or behaviors caused problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I don’t love admitting that, because I’ve always been pretty reliable at work. Like, annoyingly reliable. The person people send the messy handoff to because I’ll make a spreadsheet out of the chaos.\n\nBut my last performance review was the first one where they actually said I was missing key handoffs, leaving meetings abruptly, making the team work around me. And I wanted to argue with the wording, of course, because “abruptly” can mean a lot of things, but… they weren’t wrong.\n\nAnd at home, my husband says it feels like I’m barely present anymore. I’ll be sitting there while he’s talking, and apparently I’m just… not really there. Which is hard, because I am there, physically. But I guess not in the way he means.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these impulsive actions and avoidance behaviors?\n\nPatient: Um… since the cutover. The late-night one.\n\nI keep wanting to say it started “around then,” because that sounds less dramatic, but honestly it’s been pretty much since that incident. A little over a year now—about 13 months, I think. Which is annoying, because in project terms 13 months is, like, enough time for three reorganizations and a new ticketing system nobody asked for.\n\nBut yeah. The avoiding things, doing weird detours, acting on that impulse to get away from reminders… that’s been going on since then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you try to push away or avoid thoughts and memories related to that night when they come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t sit there and say, “Okay, now I’m avoiding a memory,” but that’s basically what happens.\n\nIf something makes me think about that night, I try to get away from it. I’ll take the long way around the building just so I don’t have to go near that area, which is ridiculous because it adds time and then I’m annoyed at myself for being late to something I could’ve reached in two minutes. It’s like when the parking garage elevators are down and everyone suddenly becomes an urban planner, except I’m doing it on purpose.\n\nAnd I avoid that floor whenever I can. If someone starts talking about the contractor, or the cutover, I’ll shut the conversation down fast. Change the subject, say I have another call, pretend I need to check something. I just don’t want it getting started in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms or behaviors caused problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I don’t love admitting that, because I’ve always been pretty reliable at work. Like, annoyingly reliable. The person people send the messy handoff to because I’ll make a spreadsheet out of the chaos.\n\nBut my last performance review was the first one where they actually said I was missing key handoffs, leaving meetings abruptly, making the team work around me. And I wanted to argue with the wording, of course, because “abruptly” can mean a lot of things, but… they weren’t wrong.\n\nAnd at home, my husband says it feels like I’m barely present anymore. I’ll be sitting there while he’s talking, and apparently I’m just… not really there. Which is hard, because I am there, physically. But I guess not in the way he means.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these impulsive actions and avoidance behaviors?\n\nPatient: Um… since the cutover. The late-night one.\n\nI keep wanting to say it started “around then,” because that sounds less dramatic, but honestly it’s been pretty much since that incident. A little over a year now—about 13 months, I think. Which is annoying, because in project terms 13 months is, like, enough time for three reorganizations and a new ticketing system nobody asked for.\n\nBut yeah. The avoiding things, doing weird detours, acting on that impulse to get away from reminders… that’s been going on since then.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have any physical reactions, such as a racing heart or sweating, when you're reminded of that night?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / computer_systems_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think I’m, like, drenched in sweat or anything obvious like that.

Sometimes I realize I’m tense after the fact, but I don’t always track the physical stuff in the moment. I’m usually more focused on getting out of the conversation or away from whatever reminded me. It’s funny—not funny, but you know what I mean—my smartwatch is always yelling at me to stand up or breathe, and I mostly ignore it because half the time it thinks I’m exercising when I’m just walking fast to a meeting.

So… racing heart? Maybe? I haven’t really noticed it clearly enough to say.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I’ve started doing things that honestly scare me. Like, I’ll just walk out in the middle of a deployment at work, which is insane because I’m usually the person who stays until everything is stable. Or I’ll get in my car late at night and just drive around with no plan.\n\nI know that probably sounds kind of random, but I’ve always been pretty controlled, you know? I work in systems, so planning is kind of my whole thing. This is just… not how I usually handle stress, and it’s starting to make me nervous.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious before or while doing these impulsive actions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but it’s hard to separate. It’s not like I calmly decide, “I’m going to do something reckless now.” It’s more like I get this pressure building, and then suddenly I’m walking out during a deployment or I’m in the car late at night just driving with no plan.\n\nAnd that’s what scares me, because I’m not usually like that. I’m usually the person with the checklist and the backup plan and, you know, three tabs open to monitor everything. So while it’s happening I feel kind of wound up and not fully… I don’t know, not fully in control of it.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding anything or any situation when you do these impulsive actions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I am. I mean, not always in the obvious way, but I’ll take the long way around the building so I don’t have to pass certain areas. And I avoid that floor whenever I can.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous saying it out loud, because it’s just a floor, right? I used to go up and down there constantly for work. But now I’ll make excuses or suddenly remember I need coffee, or I’ll use the stairs on the other side. And if people start talking about the contractor or the cutover, I kind of… shut it down. I change the subject or leave the conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, I don’t sit there trying to think about it, but it just kind of barges in.\n\nEspecially if I’m near that conference area, or if someone on a call suddenly raises their voice—like not even at me, just that sharp tone people get when production is down—I’ll suddenly get these images and sounds from that night. It’s like they’re right there for a second, and I lose the thread of what I was saying.\n\nWhich is… embarrassing, honestly, because I’ll be mid-sentence about a ticket or some server issue, and then I’m just blank. People probably think I’m distracted, but it’s not that. It’s more like my brain yanks me somewhere else before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving that night, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes it really does feel like that, or close to it. It’s not like I’m choosing to remember it.\n\nI’ll be near that conference area, or someone on a call raises their voice—people do that all the time in tech when something breaks, you know, like suddenly everyone’s talking over each other—and then out of nowhere I get hit with the images and the sounds from that night. And for a second it’s so strong that I’m not really in the meeting anymore.\n\nI’ll just lose what I was saying. Like I’ll be explaining something normal, some ticket or system issue, and then it’s just… gone. I have to kind of pretend I was checking my notes or something. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about that night that you're trying to avoid thinking about or remembering?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there is. I try not to think about that whole stretch of it—the contractor, the cutover, all of that. Even just people bringing it up makes me want to get out of the conversation.\n\nI’ll do stupid little things, like take the long way around the building so I don’t have to pass by that area, or avoid that floor if I can. I know that sounds like I’m making a big production out of it, but I don’t even always notice I’m doing it until afterward. Like, suddenly I’ve gone all the way around just to avoid being near it.\n\nAnd if someone starts talking about that night, or the project, I kind of shut it down. Change the subject, remember I have an email to send, anything. I just don’t want it in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. I mean, I’ve definitely felt really uncomfortable suddenly, but “peaked within minutes” is… I don’t know, I don’t really time it.\n\nSometimes when something happens, I get thrown off pretty fast, but then I start thinking about whether I looked weird or if I need to get back to what I was doing, and then I’m worrying about the meeting itself. Sorry, that’s probably not answering it clearly. I guess I haven’t noticed a specific sudden episode like that, not in the way you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsive actions and avoidance behaviors are directly related to something specific that happened, or are they more about general feelings of anxiety or stress?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more tied to that specific thing, yeah. I mean, I get stressed like anyone does—work is always some disaster with systems and deadlines, and people act like everything is on fire because a server hiccuped—but this feels different.\n\nIt’s like when it gets near anything about the contractor or the cutover, I just… I don’t want to be anywhere near it. I’ll take the long way around the building without even fully deciding to, or avoid that floor if I can. And if someone starts talking about it, I shut it down pretty fast. Change the subject, find a reason to leave, whatever.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess it’s not just general anxiety. It’s connected to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself do things, when you're having these impulsive episodes or avoiding certain situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I’m watching myself from the outside. I mean, sometimes afterward I’ll think, “Why did I do that?” like why did I walk all the way around instead of just going where I needed to go, but that’s more me realizing it later.\n\nI’m usually still… in myself, I guess. Just trying to get through the moment or get away from the conversation. Though, honestly, when you say “outside my body,” I start thinking of those weird corporate training videos where they tell you to “observe your behavior objectively,” which always sounds nice until you’re actually in the middle of something and you’re just trying not to look strange.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the event from that night, like you're back in it again?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like I completely forget where I am, exactly, but out of nowhere I’ll get hit with it.\n\nIt happens more in that conference area, or if someone raises their voice on a call. Which is funny—well, not funny, but you know, people on calls raise their voices all the time, especially when something breaks and everyone suddenly becomes an expert in infrastructure. But then I’ll hear it a certain way, and I get these images and sounds from that night, really strong, like they’re right there.\n\nAnd I’ll lose track of what I was saying. Like mid-sentence, just gone for a second. Then I’m trying to cover it, because nobody wants the systems analyst freezing up in the middle of a status update. But yes, I guess that is kind of like being back in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel numb or disconnected from yourself or the world around you when you're experiencing these memories or impulses?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Numb” maybe isn’t the word I’d use… or maybe I’m overthinking the word, which I do a lot. My job is basically getting paid to argue with vague terminology, so if someone says “disconnected,” my brain wants a requirements document.\n\nI don’t think I feel cut off from the world, exactly. I’m aware of where I am, who’s around, what I’m supposed to be doing. Sometimes I might get quiet or kind of… blank for a minute, but I don’t know if that’s numbness or just me trying not to react in front of people.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed feeling disconnected from myself, not clearly. It’s more like I’m trying to keep things contained.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to push away or avoid thoughts and memories related to that night when they come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t sit there and say, “Okay, now I’m avoiding a memory,” but that’s basically what happens.\n\nIf something makes me think about that night, I try to get away from it. Yeah… not like I think I’m literally there, exactly. I know I’m in the office, or on a call, or wherever.

So while it’s happening I feel kind of wound up and not fully… I don’t know, not fully in control of it.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding anything or any situation when you do these impulsive actions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I am. I mean, not always in the obvious way, but I’ll take the long way around the building so I don’t have to pass certain areas. And I avoid that floor whenever I can.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous saying it out loud, because it’s just a floor, right? I used to go up and down there constantly for work. But now I’ll make excuses or suddenly remember I need coffee, or I’ll use the stairs on the other side. And if people start talking about the contractor or the cutover, I kind of… shut it down. I change the subject or leave the conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, I don’t sit there trying to think about it, but it just kind of barges in.\n\nEspecially if I’m near that conference area, or if someone on a call suddenly raises their voice—like not even at me, just that sharp tone people get when production is down—I’ll suddenly get these images and sounds from that night. It’s like they’re right there for a second, and I lose the thread of what I was saying.\n\nWhich is… embarrassing, honestly, because I’ll be mid-sentence about a ticket or some server issue, and then I’m just blank. People probably think I’m distracted, but it’s not that. It’s more like my brain yanks me somewhere else before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving that night, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes it really does feel like that, or close to it. It’s not like I’m choosing to remember it.\n\nI’ll be near that conference area, or someone on a call raises their voice—people do that all the time in tech when something breaks, you know, like suddenly everyone’s talking over each other—and then out of nowhere I get hit with the images and the sounds from that night. And for a second it’s so strong that I’m not really in the meeting anymore.\n\nI’ll just lose what I was saying. Like I’ll be explaining something normal, some ticket or system issue, and then it’s just… gone. I have to kind of pretend I was checking my notes or something. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about that night that you're trying to avoid thinking about or remembering?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there is. I try not to think about that whole stretch of it—the contractor, the cutover, all of that. Even just people bringing it up makes me want to get out of the conversation.\n\nI’ll do stupid little things, like take the long way around the building so I don’t have to pass by that area, or avoid that floor if I can. I know that sounds like I’m making a big production out of it, but I don’t even always notice I’m doing it until afterward. Like, suddenly I’ve gone all the way around just to avoid being near it.\n\nAnd if someone starts talking about that night, or the project, I kind of shut it down. Change the subject, remember I have an email to send, anything. I just don’t want it in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. I mean, I’ve definitely felt really uncomfortable suddenly, but “peaked within minutes” is… I don’t know, I don’t really time it.\n\nSometimes when something happens, I get thrown off pretty fast, but then I start thinking about whether I looked weird or if I need to get back to what I was doing, and then I’m worrying about the meeting itself. Sorry, that’s probably not answering it clearly. I guess I haven’t noticed a specific sudden episode like that, not in the way you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsive actions and avoidance behaviors are directly related to something specific that happened, or are they more about general feelings of anxiety or stress?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more tied to that specific thing, yeah. I mean, I get stressed like anyone does—work is always some disaster with systems and deadlines, and people act like everything is on fire because a server hiccuped—but this feels different.\n\nIt’s like when it gets near anything about the contractor or the cutover, I just… I don’t want to be anywhere near it. I’ll take the long way around the building without even fully deciding to, or avoid that floor if I can. And if someone starts talking about it, I shut it down pretty fast. Change the subject, find a reason to leave, whatever.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess it’s not just general anxiety. It’s connected to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself do things, when you're having these impulsive episodes or avoiding certain situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I’m watching myself from the outside. I mean, sometimes afterward I’ll think, “Why did I do that?” like why did I walk all the way around instead of just going where I needed to go, but that’s more me realizing it later.\n\nI’m usually still… in myself, I guess. Just trying to get through the moment or get away from the conversation. Though, honestly, when you say “outside my body,” I start thinking of those weird corporate training videos where they tell you to “observe your behavior objectively,” which always sounds nice until you’re actually in the middle of something and you’re just trying not to look strange.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the event from that night, like you're back in it again?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like I completely forget where I am, exactly, but out of nowhere I’ll get hit with it.\n\nIt happens more in that conference area, or if someone raises their voice on a call. Which is funny—well, not funny, but you know, people on calls raise their voices all the time, especially when something breaks and everyone suddenly becomes an expert in infrastructure. But then I’ll hear it a certain way, and I get these images and sounds from that night, really strong, like they’re right there.\n\nAnd I’ll lose track of what I was saying. Like mid-sentence, just gone for a second. Then I’m trying to cover it, because nobody wants the systems analyst freezing up in the middle of a status update. But yes, I guess that is kind of like being back in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel numb or disconnected from yourself or the world around you when you're experiencing these memories or impulses?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Numb” maybe isn’t the word I’d use… or maybe I’m overthinking the word, which I do a lot. My job is basically getting paid to argue with vague terminology, so if someone says “disconnected,” my brain wants a requirements document.\n\nI don’t think I feel cut off from the world, exactly. I’m aware of where I am, who’s around, what I’m supposed to be doing. Sometimes I might get quiet or kind of… blank for a minute, but I don’t know if that’s numbness or just me trying not to react in front of people.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed feeling disconnected from myself, not clearly. It’s more like I’m trying to keep things contained.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to push away or avoid thoughts and memories related to that night when they come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t sit there and say, “Okay, now I’m avoiding a memory,” but that’s basically what happens.\n\nIf something makes me think about that night, I try to get away from it. I’ll take the long way around the building just so I don’t have to go near that area, which is ridiculous because it adds time and then I’m annoyed at myself for being late to something I could’ve reached in two minutes. It’s like when the parking garage elevators are down and everyone suddenly becomes an urban planner, except I’m doing it on purpose.\n\nAnd I avoid that floor whenever I can. If someone starts talking about the contractor, or the cutover, I’ll shut the conversation down fast. Change the subject, say I have another call, pretend I need to check something. I just don’t want it getting started in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms or behaviors caused problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I don’t love admitting that, because I’ve always been pretty reliable at work. Like, annoyingly reliable. The person people send the messy handoff to because I’ll make a spreadsheet out of the chaos.\n\nBut my last performance review was the first one where they actually said I was missing key handoffs, leaving meetings abruptly, making the team work around me. The late-night one.\n\nI keep wanting to say it started “around then,” because that sounds less dramatic, but honestly it’s been pretty much since that incident. A little over a year now—about 13 months, I think. Which is annoying, because in project terms 13 months is, like, enough time for three reorganizations and a new ticketing system nobody asked for.\n\nBut yeah. The avoiding things, doing weird detours, acting on that impulse to get away from reminders… that’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any physical reactions, such as a racing heart or sweating, when you're reminded of that night?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I’m usually more focused on getting out of the conversation or away from whatever reminded me. It’s funny—not funny, but you know what I mean—my smartwatch is always yelling at me to stand up or breathe, and I mostly ignore it because half the time it thinks I’m exercising when I’m just walking fast to a meeting.\n\nSo… racing heart? I haven’t really noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're back in the situation from that night, reliving it as if it's happening again?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not like I think I’m literally there, exactly. Yeah… it has. More than I wanted to admit, honestly.

Even just people bringing it up makes me want to get out of the conversation.\n\nI’ll do stupid little things, like take the long way around the building so I don’t have to pass by that area, or avoid that floor if I can. I know that sounds like I’m making a big production out of it, but I don’t even always notice I’m doing it until afterward. Like, suddenly I’ve gone all the way around just to avoid being near it.\n\nAnd if someone starts talking about that night, or the project, I kind of shut it down. Change the subject, remember I have an email to send, anything. I just don’t want it in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. I mean, I’ve definitely felt really uncomfortable suddenly, but “peaked within minutes” is… I don’t know, I don’t really time it.\n\nSometimes when something happens, I get thrown off pretty fast, but then I start thinking about whether I looked weird or if I need to get back to what I was doing, and then I’m worrying about the meeting itself. Sorry, that’s probably not answering it clearly. I guess I haven’t noticed a specific sudden episode like that, not in the way you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsive actions and avoidance behaviors are directly related to something specific that happened, or are they more about general feelings of anxiety or stress?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more tied to that specific thing, yeah. I mean, I get stressed like anyone does—work is always some disaster with systems and deadlines, and people act like everything is on fire because a server hiccuped—but this feels different.\n\nIt’s like when it gets near anything about the contractor or the cutover, I just… I don’t want to be anywhere near it. I’ll take the long way around the building without even fully deciding to, or avoid that floor if I can. And if someone starts talking about it, I shut it down pretty fast. Change the subject, find a reason to leave, whatever.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess it’s not just general anxiety. It’s connected to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself do things, when you're having these impulsive episodes or avoiding certain situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I’m watching myself from the outside. I mean, sometimes afterward I’ll think, “Why did I do that?” like why did I walk all the way around instead of just going where I needed to go, but that’s more me realizing it later.\n\nI’m usually still… in myself, I guess. Just trying to get through the moment or get away from the conversation. Though, honestly, when you say “outside my body,” I start thinking of those weird corporate training videos where they tell you to “observe your behavior objectively,” which always sounds nice until you’re actually in the middle of something and you’re just trying not to look strange.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the event from that night, like you're back in it again?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like I completely forget where I am, exactly, but out of nowhere I’ll get hit with it.\n\nIt happens more in that conference area, or if someone raises their voice on a call. Which is funny—well, not funny, but you know, people on calls raise their voices all the time, especially when something breaks and everyone suddenly becomes an expert in infrastructure. But then I’ll hear it a certain way, and I get these images and sounds from that night, really strong, like they’re right there.\n\nAnd I’ll lose track of what I was saying. Like mid-sentence, just gone for a second. Then I’m trying to cover it, because nobody wants the systems analyst freezing up in the middle of a status update. But yes, I guess that is kind of like being back in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel numb or disconnected from yourself or the world around you when you're experiencing these memories or impulses?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Numb” maybe isn’t the word I’d use… or maybe I’m overthinking the word, which I do a lot. My job is basically getting paid to argue with vague terminology, so if someone says “disconnected,” my brain wants a requirements document.\n\nI don’t think I feel cut off from the world, exactly. I’m aware of where I am, who’s around, what I’m supposed to be doing. Sometimes I might get quiet or kind of… blank for a minute, but I don’t know if that’s numbness or just me trying not to react in front of people.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed feeling disconnected from myself, not clearly. It’s more like I’m trying to keep things contained.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to push away or avoid thoughts and memories related to that night when they come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t sit there and say, “Okay, now I’m avoiding a memory,” but that’s basically what happens.\n\nIf something makes me think about that night, I try to get away from it. I’ll take the long way around the building just so I don’t have to go near that area, which is ridiculous because it adds time and then I’m annoyed at myself for being late to something I could’ve reached in two minutes. It’s like when the parking garage elevators are down and everyone suddenly becomes an urban planner, except I’m doing it on purpose.\n\nAnd I avoid that floor whenever I can. If someone starts talking about the contractor, or the cutover, I’ll shut the conversation down fast. Change the subject, say I have another call, pretend I need to check something. I just don’t want it getting started in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms or behaviors caused problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I don’t love admitting that, because I’ve always been pretty reliable at work. Like, annoyingly reliable. The person people send the messy handoff to because I’ll make a spreadsheet out of the chaos.\n\nBut my last performance review was the first one where they actually said I was missing key handoffs, leaving meetings abruptly, making the team work around me. And I wanted to argue with the wording, of course, because “abruptly” can mean a lot of things, but… they weren’t wrong.\n\nAnd at home, my husband says it feels like I’m barely present anymore. I’ll be sitting there while he’s talking, and apparently I’m just… not really there. Which is hard, because I am there, physically. But I guess not in the way he means.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these impulsive actions and avoidance behaviors?\n\nPatient: Um… since the cutover. The late-night one.\n\nI keep wanting to say it started “around then,” because that sounds less dramatic, but honestly it’s been pretty much since that incident. A little over a year now—about 13 months, I think. Which is annoying, because in project terms 13 months is, like, enough time for three reorganizations and a new ticketing system nobody asked for.\n\nBut yeah. The avoiding things, doing weird detours, acting on that impulse to get away from reminders… that’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any physical reactions, such as a racing heart or sweating, when you're reminded of that night?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think I’m, like, drenched in sweat or anything obvious like that.\n\nSometimes I realize I’m tense after the fact, but I don’t always track the physical stuff in the moment. I’m usually more focused on getting out of the conversation or away from whatever reminded me. It’s funny—not funny, but you know what I mean—my smartwatch is always yelling at me to stand up or breathe, and I mostly ignore it because half the time it thinks I’m exercising when I’m just walking fast to a meeting.\n\nSo… racing heart? Maybe? I haven’t really noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're back in the situation from that night, reliving it as if it's happening again?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not like I think I’m literally there, exactly. I know I’m in the office, or on a call, or wherever.\n\nBut out of nowhere—especially near that conference area, or if someone raises their voice on a call—I’ll get these images and sounds from that night, really strong. Like they cut in over whatever is happening now. I’ll be mid-sentence, and suddenly I’m not following what I was saying anymore.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because then I’m just staring at a slide or someone’s name in Teams, trying to remember the thread. And of course Teams always does that thing where it freezes on the worst possible facial expression, so part of me is noticing that too, which is ridiculous, but… yeah. For a few seconds, it feels like that night is right on top of the present.\n\nDoctor: Have these experiences and behaviors significantly impacted your ability to function in your daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. More than I wanted to admit, honestly.\n\nWork is where it got kind of undeniable. My last performance review was the first one where they actually said I was missing key handoffs, leaving meetings abruptly, and basically making the team work around me. Which—ugh—I hate that phrase, because I’m usually the person fixing the workflow, not becoming the workflow problem.\n\nAnd at home… my husband says it feels like I’m barely present anymore. I’ll be sitting there, technically in the room, but not really there with him. I keep thinking if I just organize things better, or make a better list—because that’s my solution to everything, apparently—but it’s not really fixing it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not with, like, every sense exactly. I don’t smell things or feel the room change or anything that dramatic.

But I do get hit with it, if that makes sense. Especially around that conference area, or if someone raises their voice on a call—suddenly I’ll have these images and sounds from that night really strongly, like they just cut in. I’ll be talking and then I’ve completely lost what I was saying.

I mean, I’ve definitely felt really uncomfortable suddenly, but “peaked within minutes” is… I don’t know, I don’t really time it.\n\nSometimes when something happens, I get thrown off pretty fast, but then I start thinking about whether I looked weird or if I need to get back to what I was doing, and then I’m worrying about the meeting itself. Sorry, that’s probably not answering it clearly. I guess I haven’t noticed a specific sudden episode like that, not in the way you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsive actions and avoidance behaviors are directly related to something specific that happened, or are they more about general feelings of anxiety or stress?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more tied to that specific thing, yeah. I mean, I get stressed like anyone does—work is always some disaster with systems and deadlines, and people act like everything is on fire because a server hiccuped—but this feels different.\n\nIt’s like when it gets near anything about the contractor or the cutover, I just… I don’t want to be anywhere near it. I’ll take the long way around the building without even fully deciding to, or avoid that floor if I can. And if someone starts talking about it, I shut it down pretty fast. Change the subject, find a reason to leave, whatever.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess it’s not just general anxiety. It’s connected to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself do things, when you're having these impulsive episodes or avoiding certain situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I’m watching myself from the outside. I mean, sometimes afterward I’ll think, “Why did I do that?” like why did I walk all the way around instead of just going where I needed to go, but that’s more me realizing it later.\n\nI’m usually still… in myself, I guess. Just trying to get through the moment or get away from the conversation. Though, honestly, when you say “outside my body,” I start thinking of those weird corporate training videos where they tell you to “observe your behavior objectively,” which always sounds nice until you’re actually in the middle of something and you’re just trying not to look strange.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the event from that night, like you're back in it again?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like I completely forget where I am, exactly, but out of nowhere I’ll get hit with it.\n\nIt happens more in that conference area, or if someone raises their voice on a call. Which is funny—well, not funny, but you know, people on calls raise their voices all the time, especially when something breaks and everyone suddenly becomes an expert in infrastructure. But then I’ll hear it a certain way, and I get these images and sounds from that night, really strong, like they’re right there.\n\nAnd I’ll lose track of what I was saying. Like mid-sentence, just gone for a second. Then I’m trying to cover it, because nobody wants the systems analyst freezing up in the middle of a status update. But yes, I guess that is kind of like being back in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel numb or disconnected from yourself or the world around you when you're experiencing these memories or impulses?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Numb” maybe isn’t the word I’d use… or maybe I’m overthinking the word, which I do a lot. My job is basically getting paid to argue with vague terminology, so if someone says “disconnected,” my brain wants a requirements document.\n\nI don’t think I feel cut off from the world, exactly. I’m aware of where I am, who’s around, what I’m supposed to be doing. Sometimes I might get quiet or kind of… blank for a minute, but I don’t know if that’s numbness or just me trying not to react in front of people.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed feeling disconnected from myself, not clearly. It’s more like I’m trying to keep things contained.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to push away or avoid thoughts and memories related to that night when they come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t sit there and say, “Okay, now I’m avoiding a memory,” but that’s basically what happens.\n\nIf something makes me think about that night, I try to get away from it. I’ll take the long way around the building just so I don’t have to go near that area, which is ridiculous because it adds time and then I’m annoyed at myself for being late to something I could’ve reached in two minutes. It’s like when the parking garage elevators are down and everyone suddenly becomes an urban planner, except I’m doing it on purpose.\n\nAnd I avoid that floor whenever I can. If someone starts talking about the contractor, or the cutover, I’ll shut the conversation down fast. Change the subject, say I have another call, pretend I need to check something. I just don’t want it getting started in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms or behaviors caused problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I don’t love admitting that, because I’ve always been pretty reliable at work. Like, annoyingly reliable. The person people send the messy handoff to because I’ll make a spreadsheet out of the chaos.\n\nBut my last performance review was the first one where they actually said I was missing key handoffs, leaving meetings abruptly, making the team work around me. And I wanted to argue with the wording, of course, because “abruptly” can mean a lot of things, but… they weren’t wrong.\n\nAnd at home, my husband says it feels like I’m barely present anymore. I’ll be sitting there while he’s talking, and apparently I’m just… not really there. Which is hard, because I am there, physically. But I guess not in the way he means.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these impulsive actions and avoidance behaviors?\n\nPatient: Um… since the cutover. The late-night one.\n\nI keep wanting to say it started “around then,” because that sounds less dramatic, but honestly it’s been pretty much since that incident. A little over a year now—about 13 months, I think. Which is annoying, because in project terms 13 months is, like, enough time for three reorganizations and a new ticketing system nobody asked for.\n\nBut yeah. The avoiding things, doing weird detours, acting on that impulse to get away from reminders… that’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any physical reactions, such as a racing heart or sweating, when you're reminded of that night?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think I’m, like, drenched in sweat or anything obvious like that.\n\nSometimes I realize I’m tense after the fact, but I don’t always track the physical stuff in the moment. I’m usually more focused on getting out of the conversation or away from whatever reminded me. It’s funny—not funny, but you know what I mean—my smartwatch is always yelling at me to stand up or breathe, and I mostly ignore it because half the time it thinks I’m exercising when I’m just walking fast to a meeting.\n\nSo… racing heart? Maybe? I haven’t really noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're back in the situation from that night, reliving it as if it's happening again?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not like I think I’m literally there, exactly. I know I’m in the office, or on a call, or wherever.\n\nBut out of nowhere—especially near that conference area, or if someone raises their voice on a call—I’ll get these images and sounds from that night, really strong. Like they cut in over whatever is happening now. I’ll be mid-sentence, and suddenly I’m not following what I was saying anymore.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because then I’m just staring at a slide or someone’s name in Teams, trying to remember the thread. And of course Teams always does that thing where it freezes on the worst possible facial expression, so part of me is noticing that too, which is ridiculous, but… yeah. For a few seconds, it feels like that night is right on top of the present.\n\nDoctor: Have these experiences and behaviors significantly impacted your ability to function in your daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. More than I wanted to admit, honestly.\n\nWork is where it got kind of undeniable. My last performance review was the first one where they actually said I was missing key handoffs, leaving meetings abruptly, and basically making the team work around me. Which—ugh—I hate that phrase, because I’m usually the person fixing the workflow, not becoming the workflow problem.\n\nAnd at home… my husband says it feels like I’m barely present anymore. I’ll be sitting there, technically in the room, but not really there with him. I keep thinking if I just organize things better, or make a better list—because that’s my solution to everything, apparently—but it’s not really fixing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're back in the exact situation from that night, reliving it as if it's happening all over again, with all your senses?\n\nPatient: Not with, like, every sense exactly. I don’t smell things or feel the room change or anything that dramatic.\n\nBut I do get hit with it, if that makes sense. Especially around that conference area, or if someone raises their voice on a call—suddenly I’ll have these images and sounds from that night really strongly, like they just cut in. I’ll be talking and then I’ve completely lost what I was saying.\n\nIt’s not that I believe I’m back there, but for a few seconds it feels way too close. Then I’m trying to act normal and pick up the sentence again, which is… not always successful."
